It’s like everything I tried to rebuild after the divorce has sort of collapsed, and I’m just sitting underneath it. I used to like doing little projects around the apartment, or going out for coffee with someone, but I haven’t really thought about whether I “enjoy” them now. I guess I still do some things because I should, not necessarily because I’m excited about them. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific activity or hobby that you used to really enjoy, and tell me if you still get the same pleasure out of it now?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know I’m probably not answering this very clearly. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately—like eating significantly less, not feeling hungry, or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in a way I’ve really tracked. My appetite is maybe a little inconsistent, but I can’t say I’ve lost weight without trying or anything obvious like that. I’m sorry, I know that’s not very precise. I just haven’t noticed a clear change there.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even things that were once meaningful or fun for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I still can enjoy things sometimes, I think, so I don’t want to overstate it. But I guess I haven’t really been seeking things out the way I used to. It’s more like I do what I’m supposed to do, and if something nice happens, maybe it’s okay, but I’m not really looking forward to much.\n\nIs that what you mean by loss of interest, or does it have to be more extreme than that?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the past few weeks when you felt genuinely interested or engaged in something? If not, has this lack of interest affected your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a good example… I mean, there are moments where I’m not miserable or anything. Like if someone at work asks me a technical question, I can sort of click into it for a bit. But genuinely interested, like I’m excited or absorbed? I’m not sure.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’m still showing up, and I’m still polite with people. But I guess I’ve been more… passive? Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not interested in things you used to care about, or that activities you used to enjoy don’t bring you any satisfaction at all—even a little bit?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “no satisfaction at all.” That sounds a little stronger than what I’m sure of.\n\nIt’s more like… I don’t really reach for those things, and when I do, they don’t always feel worth the effort. Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there’s been a time in the past two weeks when you felt no pleasure or interest in anything at all—even things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can honestly say “no pleasure or interest in anything at all.” There have been times where I felt pretty flat, or like nothing seemed especially appealing, but if I’m being precise, I don’t know that it was absolutely everything.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s kind of a cautious answer. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not enjoying things that used to make you happy, like hobbies, time with friends, or other activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean… I still try to do the things I’m supposed to do, and I don’t want to sound like I’m just complaining. Maybe some things don’t feel as easy or as fun as they used to, but I haven’t really been paying close attention to it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any activity or hobby that you used to enjoy, and tell me if you still find pleasure in it now?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure. I used to like doing little projects around the apartment, or going out for coffee with someone, but I haven’t really thought about whether I “enjoy” them now. I guess I still do some things because I should, not necessarily because I’m excited about them. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific activity or hobby that you used to really enjoy, and tell me if you still get the same pleasure out of it now?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know I’m probably not answering this very clearly. I can think of things I used to like, but it’s hard for me to tell if I’m getting the same pleasure from them now. I haven’t really been checking in with myself that way. I guess I mostly just… go through the motions and try to keep up. Is that an okay answer?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of one specific thing you used to really enjoy—like a hobby, activity, or time with someone—and tell me what it feels like when you do it now, if you still do it at all?\n\nPatient: I wish I had a cleaner answer. Maybe… coffee with a friend is the easiest example, because I know I used to look forward to that. Now if I go, I’m mostly trying to act normal and keep the conversation going. I don’t know if that means I’m not enjoying it, exactly, or if I’m just distracted or tired. It’s not terrible. It’s just… kind of muted, I guess. Is that specific enough?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that most days, you feel down or sad for most of the day, even when there’s no particular reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but that’s kind of why I came in. I just can’t shake this heavy, hopeless feeling most of the time, like everything I tried to rebuild after the divorce has collapsed and I’m just… sitting under it. Even when nothing specific is happening, it’s there. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re feeling more tired or lacking energy, even when you’re not doing much physically?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed that. I mean, I’m still getting through things, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely nonfunctional, but a normal workday feels like… dragging wet concrete or something. And even small things, like taking a shower or just opening my laptop, feel like they take more energy than I seem to have. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately—like eating significantly less, not feeling hungry, or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in a way I’ve really tracked. My appetite is maybe a little inconsistent, but I can’t say I’ve lost weight without trying or anything obvious like that. I’m sorry, I know that’s not very precise. I just haven’t noticed a clear change there.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even things that were once meaningful or fun for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I still can enjoy things sometimes, I think, so I don’t want to overstate it. But I guess I haven’t really been seeking things out the way I used to. It’s more like I do what I’m supposed to do, and if something nice happens, maybe it’s okay, but I’m not really looking forward to much.\n\nIs that what you mean by loss of interest, or does it have to be more extreme than that?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the past few weeks when you felt genuinely interested or engaged in something? If not, has this lack of interest affected your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a good example… I mean, there are moments where I’m not miserable or anything. Like if someone at work asks me a technical question, I can sort of click into it for a bit. But genuinely interested, like I’m excited or absorbed? I’m not sure.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’m still showing up, and I’m still polite with people. But I guess I’ve been more… passive? Like I don’t really initiate plans, and I probably answer messages later than I should. I don’t know if that counts as affecting relationships, but I can see how it might. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not interested in things you used to care about, or that activities you used to enjoy don’t bring you any satisfaction at all—even a little bit?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “no satisfaction at all.” That sounds a little stronger than what I’m sure of.\n\nIt’s more like… I don’t really reach for those things, and when I do, they don’t always feel worth the effort. But I can still get a little something from things sometimes, I think. Maybe not as much as before. I’m sorry, I’m trying to be accurate and not make it sound dramatic.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve completely stopped doing or thinking about doing things you used to care about, even small things, because they just don’t seem worth it or interesting anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve completely stopped, no. At least… I don’t want to say that if it isn’t exactly true.\n\nThere are small things I still think about doing sometimes, but I don’t always follow through. It’s more like they feel kind of optional or like they’d take more energy than they’re worth in the moment. But “completely stopped” feels too absolute. I still care a little, I think. Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there’s been a time in the past two weeks when you felt no pleasure or interest in anything at all—even things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can honestly say “no pleasure or interest in anything at all.” There have been times where I felt pretty flat, or like nothing seemed especially appealing, but if I’m being precise, I don’t know that it was absolutely everything.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s kind of a cautious answer. I just don’t want to overstate it. It’s more like things don’t pull me in the way they used to, but “nothing at all” feels hard for me to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your mood has been consistently down or sad for most of the day, nearly every day, over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s kind of why I came in.\n\nIt’s been this heavy, hopeless feeling I can’t really shake. There have been days where I felt really disconnected from things I normally care about, like they just didn’t grab me or seem worth starting.\n\nBut completely uninteresting or unenjoyable… I don’t know. I feel like I keep hesitating because I don’t want to answer it wrong. It’s more that things feel muted, I guess, like I can’t get much out of them even when I know I used to. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific activity or hobby that you used to really enjoy, and tell me if you still get the same pleasure out of it now?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know I’m probably not answering this very clearly. I can think of things I used to like, but it’s hard for me to tell if I’m getting the same pleasure from them now. I haven’t really been checking in with myself that way. I guess I mostly just… go through the motions and try to keep up. Is that an okay answer?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of one specific thing you used to really enjoy—like a hobby, activity, or time with someone—and tell me what it feels like when you do it now, if you still do it at all?\n\nPatient: I wish I had a cleaner answer. Maybe… coffee with a friend is the easiest example, because I know I used to look forward to that. Now if I go, I’m mostly trying to act normal and keep the conversation going. I don’t know if that means I’m not enjoying it, exactly, or if I’m just distracted or tired. It’s not terrible. It’s just… kind of muted, I guess. Is that specific enough?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that most days, you feel down or sad for most of the day, even when there’s no particular reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but that’s kind of why I came in. I just can’t shake this heavy, hopeless feeling most of the time, like everything I tried to rebuild after the divorce has collapsed and I’m just… sitting under it. Even when nothing specific is happening, it’s there. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re feeling more tired or lacking energy, even when you’re not doing much physically?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed that. I mean, I’m still getting through things, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely nonfunctional, but a normal workday feels like… dragging wet concrete or something. And even small things, like taking a shower or just opening my laptop, feel like they take more energy than I seem to have. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately—like eating significantly less, not feeling hungry, or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in a way I’ve really tracked. My appetite is maybe a little inconsistent, but I can’t say I’ve lost weight without trying or anything obvious like that. I’m sorry, I know that’s not very precise. I just haven’t noticed a clear change there.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even things that were once meaningful or fun for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I still can enjoy things sometimes, I think, so I don’t want to overstate it. But I guess I haven’t really been seeking things out the way I used to. It’s more like I do what I’m supposed to do, and if something nice happens, maybe it’s okay, but I’m not really looking forward to much.\n\nIs that what you mean by loss of interest, or does it have to be more extreme than that?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the past few weeks when you felt genuinely interested or engaged in something? If not, has this lack of interest affected your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a good example… I mean, there are moments where I’m not miserable or anything. Like if someone at work asks me a technical question, I can sort of click into it for a bit. But genuinely interested, like I’m excited or absorbed? I’m not sure.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’m still showing up, and I’m still polite with people. But I guess I’ve been more… passive? Like I don’t really initiate plans, and I probably answer messages later than I should. I don’t know if that counts as affecting relationships, but I can see how it might. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not interested in things you used to care about, or that activities you used to enjoy don’t bring you any satisfaction at all—even a little bit?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “no satisfaction at all.” That sounds a little stronger than what I’m sure of.\n\nIt’s more like… I don’t really reach for those things, and when I do, they don’t always feel worth the effort. But I can still get a little something from things sometimes, I think. Maybe not as much as before. I’m sorry, I’m trying to be accurate and not make it sound dramatic.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve completely stopped doing or thinking about doing things you used to care about, even small things, because they just don’t seem worth it or interesting anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve completely stopped, no. At least… I don’t want to say that if it isn’t exactly true.\n\nThere are small things I still think about doing sometimes, but I don’t always follow through. It’s more like they feel kind of optional or like they’d take more energy than they’re worth in the moment. But “completely stopped” feels too absolute. I still care a little, I think. Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there’s been a time in the past two weeks when you felt no pleasure or interest in anything at all—even things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can honestly say “no pleasure or interest in anything at all.” There have been times where I felt pretty flat, or like nothing seemed especially appealing, but if I’m being precise, I don’t know that it was absolutely everything.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s kind of a cautious answer. Like everything I tried to build back up after the divorce has just… collapsed, and I’m sitting under it. Most days it’s there for a lot of the day. I’m not sure if I’m answering that the right way, but yes, it’s been pretty constant.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been days when even things you used to care about felt completely uninteresting or unenjoyable, like nothing really pulled your attention or gave you any sense of pleasure at all?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “nothing” gave me pleasure. There have been days where I felt really disconnected from things I normally care about, like they just didn’t grab me or seem worth starting.\n\nBut completely uninteresting or unenjoyable… I don’t know. I feel like I keep hesitating because I don’t want to answer it wrong. It’s more that things feel muted, I guess, like I can’t get much out of them even when I know I used to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if your low mood and loss of interest in things have been present most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s been about two weeks. I mean, it’s not like I marked the exact day on a calendar, but this whole shift—feeling really out of control and then crashing hard—has been going on for around that long.\n\nThe low mood has been there most days, a lot of the day. And the interest part… yes, mostly. I keep wanting to qualify it, but I guess the honest answer is that it’s been pretty constant for about two weeks. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been days recently when even the things you used to care about the most felt completely uninteresting or gave you no sense of enjoyment at all?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer “completely.” There have been days where I just… couldn’t really get into anything, even things that normally matter to me. It felt flat, like I was going through the motions more than enjoying it.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say absolutely no enjoyment at all. Maybe that sounds like I’m splitting hairs—I just don’t want to overstate it. It’s more like I cared somewhere in the background, but I couldn’t feel much from it. Does that count?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I feel like I keep hesitating because I don’t want to answer it wrong. It’s more that things feel muted, I guess, like I can’t get much out of them even when I know I used to. It felt flat, like I was going through the motions more than enjoying it.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say absolutely no enjoyment at all. Maybe that sounds like I’m splitting hairs—I just don’t want to overstate it. It’s more like I cared somewhere in the background, but I couldn’t feel much from it. Does that count?\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you tell me if there were any days recently when nothing—absolutely nothing—felt enjoyable or interesting to you, even things you used to really like?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / civil_engineer / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. I… I don’t know if I can honestly say “absolutely nothing.” That feels so definite, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it was if that’s not accurate.
